{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"type":"text","text":"Base directory for this skill: /work/.claude/skills/jigc\n\n`jigc setup` wrote this file from jigc 1.0.0-rc.14 and owns it: re-run `jigc setup` after upgrading the binary to refresh it.\n\nIt carries the two guides that ship with that binary, one after the other — the quickstart loop, then the migration field notes. A cross-reference to `QUICKSTART.md` or `MIGRATING.md` means the matching part of this file; every other jigc document named below lives in the jigc project's own repository, not in this one, which is why none of them are links here.\n\n# Quickstart\n\n`jigc` is a context compiler for coding agents: a deterministic CLI that\nassembles exactly the instructions and document slices an agent needs for a\ntask, and is the sole channel through which the agent reads and writes managed\nproject documents. This walks the MVP loop on a real machine —\n**install → start → finalize**.\n\n> The name is **jigc** — pronounced \"jig-see\" — *a jig for coding agents*: the\n> jig holds the work and guides the tool so the cut lands true (settled\n> 2026-07-03, `DECISIONS.md` → The name is settled). The binary is the same name.\n\n## Install\n\n`jigc` ships as a single binary. Install it from the workspace and put it on\nyour `PATH`:\n\n```sh\ncargo install --path crates/cli      # or: cargo build --release -p cli\ncp target/release/jigc /usr/local/bin/   # if you built rather than installed\n```\n\nJust `jigc` — nothing else to copy. The doc↔code probe (`doc-code`) is\nembedded in the binary and extracted beside it by `jigc setup` (below), so a\n`cargo install` from a fresh machine is a supported install channel: no manual\nprobe copy, and code-anchor finalize / `jigc validate` work out of the box.\n\nConfirm it identifies itself:\n\n```sh\njigc --version          # → jigc <version>\n```\n\n## 1. `jigc setup` — adapter install\n\nRun once per project, from the repo root. `setup` generates the Claude Code\nadapter from the embedded profile and installs it idempotently\n(`design/assistant-adapter.md` → Generated, minimal, regenerated):\n\n```sh\njigc setup\n```\n\nIt does these things:\n\n- writes the managed bootstrap file `.jigc/AGENT.md` and adds a bare\n  `@.jigc/AGENT.md` import to `CLAUDE.md` (the static floor that tells the agent\n  `jigc` is its interface to the project — a plain import line, no marker fence);\n- allowlists `Bash(jigc:*)` in `.claude/settings.json` and adds a `SessionStart` hook\n  that runs `jigc start` (so each session opens with orientation), so the agent\n  can call the CLI without a permission prompt;\n- writes jigc's own guides — this file and the migration field notes — to the path\n  your assistant reads skills from (`.claude/skills/jigc/SKILL.md` for Claude Code),\n  as **one file jigc owns**: it opens with a `jigc-version:` stamp naming the build\n  that wrote it plus the hash of its own body, and every `jigc setup` rewrites it, so\n  the guidance in your repo always matches the binary in your `PATH`. Re-run `setup`\n  after upgrading and the copy follows;\n- extracts the embedded `doc-code` probe beside the installed `jigc` (so the\n  doc↔code probe resolves next to the binary — written if no sibling is present\n  **or** if an existing sibling's bytes differ from the embedded copy, so a\n  stale or corrupt probe self-heals; a byte-identical sibling is left untouched); and\n- installs a `pre-commit` hook that runs `jigc validate` over the committed\n  store and **warns** on doc↔code drift without blocking the commit — a\n  backstop for edits made outside the loop, not a gate. It refuses exactly one\n  thing: a commit that itself stages an out-of-band managed-doc **rename** (a\n  bare `git mv` of a managed doc, both paths staged), because that silently\n  breaks identity tracking — use `jigc rename` instead. Drift that landed in an\n  earlier commit only warns; it never blocks a later, unrelated one.\n\n`setup` **commits its own install** as a dedicated\n`chore(jigc): install jigc workspace config` commit, so the install doesn't land in\nyour first feature commit. It stages **only the files it wrote inside the repo** —\nnever your working tree — so two of the things above are *not* in that commit: the\n`doc-code` probe (it lives beside the `jigc` binary, not in your repo), and the\n`pre-commit` hook whenever git keeps hooks outside your working tree — the usual\n`.git/hooks/`, a `core.hooksPath` pointing elsewhere, or a linked worktree's shared\nhooks dir — because git cannot track a file there. If your repo keeps hooks *in* the\ntree (an in-repo `core.hooksPath`), the hook is an ordinary tracked file and `setup`\ncommits it with the rest — unless git will not take that path from your repo, in which\ncase `setup` leaves the hook out of its commit and commits the rest normally. That\ncovers a hooks dir belonging to **another** repository (shared hooks vendored as a\nsubmodule — checked out or not — or an embedded repo), where it is that repo's file to\ncommit, not yours, and a hooks dir your **sparse-checkout** excludes. Either way the\nhook is installed and works locally, it just isn't in the commit; note that a committed\nhook carries **this** machine's `jigc`\npath, so in someone else's clone it stays silent until they run `jigc setup`\nthemselves. These edits are idempotent — re-running `jigc setup` leaves the files\nbyte-identical (no new commit), so it is safe to run after every upgrade to re-apply\nthe adapter.\n\n## 2. `jigc start \"<intent>\"` — route, then mint\n\nHand the agent (or yourself) a task by stating the intent. `start` composes\nthe cascade-default **router** (`design/write-commands.md` → Task\norigination): it presents the selectable work-workflows with the situation\neach fits, and emits the re-run line — nothing is minted yet:\n\n```sh\njigc start \"add per-client rate limit at the gateway\"\n# → the workflow menu, then the re-run line:\n#   jigc start --workflow <chosen> \"<intent>\"\n```\n\nPick the workflow whose situation fits the intent and re-run with that\nchoice — this is the call that mints:\n\n```sh\njigc start --workflow single-task \"add per-client rate limit at the gateway\"\n```\n\nThis opens the task's working area under `.jigc/tasks/<id>/` and emits the\ncomposed workflow — the instructions plus the document slices the task needs.\nThe agent reads its intent and the codebase, implements the change directly in\nthe working tree, and (when a decision is warranted) records an ADR in-task\nthrough the workflow's `create` gate.\n\nBare `jigc start` (no intent) orients instead: it reports project state and the\nnext action, read-only.\n\n### Reading while the task is open\n\nManaged docs are read through `jigc`, never off disk — three reads cover it, and\n`--task <id>` turns the first two onto the working copies your open task has\nstaged but not yet committed:\n\n```sh\njigc doc list --task <id>             # what this task stages, each by its address\njigc doc show adr:<slug> --task <id>  # the staged doc itself\njigc doc schema adr                   # the shape a write has to fill\n```\n\nDrop `--task` and the first two serve the committed store instead. `jigc doc\nschema` needs no task — it projects the resolved schema: required slots and\nfields, each field's enum members, the value grammar of any pack-declared field\ntype, and every address a write can take.\n\nA `code-anchor` field — `adr.cites-code`, `spec.criteria/maps-to-test`,\n`arch-doc.components/implemented-by` — takes `<repo-relative-path>[#<symbol>]`:\na path from the repository root, optionally `#` and the name of a unit *declared*\nin that file (`src/router.ts#createRouter`). It is not `file:line` and not\n`Class.method`; a bare path with no `#` is legal and asserts only that the file\nexists. `jigc doc schema <doctype>` and `jigc doc set-field --help` both print the\ngrammar, so there is nothing to remember.\n\n## 3. `jigc task finalize <id>` — the commit boundary\n\nWhen the work is done, finalize. This is the single transactional boundary\n(`design/finalize.md`): it validates, renders the commit-doc into the git commit\nmessage, promotes any created ADR into `docs/decisions/`, and `git`-commits the\ncode changes plus promoted docs as one commit.\n\n```sh\njigc task finalize <id>\n```\n\nIf validation blocks (a dangling forward reference, a missing required slot, a\nmalformed value), finalize makes no commit and surfaces the findings with a\nroute for the next action. Fix and re-run.\n\nYour own `pre-commit` / `commit-msg` hooks still run — jigc never passes\n`--no-verify`, they are your policy — so a hook that rejects the commit stops\nthe finalize. Nothing is committed, the task survives — its staged docs still in\n`.jigc/tasks/<id>/docs/`, anything you had `git add`-ed still in git's index —\nand the message carries the hook's own output verbatim plus the line to re-run\nonce its complaint is fixed. Every jigc verb that commits on\nyour behalf answers a rejection that way, each stating what *its* rejection\nleft behind — the per-door detail is in MIGRATING.md →\nReconciling and backing out.\n\nA landed finalize prints a **manifest** — the file-set the commit carried (the\ngit index: what you staged plus the docs jigc promotes), with a distinct\n**left-out** list naming any unstaged or untracked work the commit excluded (so\na stray `scratch.txt` stands out as left behind, never swept in). To see that\nset *before* committing, run `jigc task finalize <id> --dry-run`: it prints the\nmanifest and stops, changing nothing. A change staged *before* the task existed\nrefuses to ride the commit (`finalize.carried-staged`) unless you declare it\nwith `--carry-staged`.\n\nChanged your mind? **`jigc task discard <id>`** abandons the task — it removes\nonly the working area under `.jigc/tasks/`; no commit is made and the committed\nstore is untouched. It **refuses first if that area stages docs no commit has a\ncopy of** — one blocking `task-discard.staged-prose` naming each of them, so you\ncan read them back (`jigc doc show <address> --task <id>`) or land them\n(`jigc task finalize <id>`) before deciding. **`--force` is the single consent**\nthat discards them along with the area. Minting a task stages its commit doc, so\nexpect that refusal on any task you have actually started. The full\nreconcile/back-out ladder (the migration review hold, the carryover gate, the\nhook rejection above, out-of-band edits) is in\nMIGRATING.md → Reconciling and backing out.\n\n**What lands in your repo:** finalize promotes each managed doc under the\n**`docs-root`** parent (default `docs/`) at its doctype's location —\n`docs/decisions/` (ADRs), `docs/specs/`, `docs/prds/`, `docs/architecture/`\n(arch-docs), `docs/milestone-records/` — as plain, human-reviewable Markdown\nalongside your own `src/`. (The names are chosen for readability, not a uniform\n`<type>s/` rule.) A few doctypes are **placed** rather than located: they live at\none literal path that `docs-root` does not move — the changelog is your\nrepo-root `CHANGELOG.md` (design/storage.md → Placement).\nPrefer a different parent, or the old flat repo-root layout? Set\n`jigc config set docs-root <path>` (`.` or `\"\"` for the old flat layout). Everything else jigc\nwrites lives under `.jigc/` (committed config + bootstrap; gitignored caches) —\nsee design/storage.md → Repository layout / Config layout. If\nyou already have a same-named dir (say an existing `docs/architecture/`), run\n`jigc ingest` first — it detects and routes existing content rather than\noverwriting it.\n\nYou can preview part of what finalize will gate on at any time — this task's\ncontent findings, the carryover gate, the `owner-artifact` causes that need no\nstaging, and the granted-but-unused changelog gate. The staged set, promotion\nand the commit itself (your hooks included) are decided at `finalize`, so a clean `validate` means *nothing this\nside of the commit blocks it*, not *this will commit*:\n\n```sh\njigc task validate <id>\njigc task diff <id>\n```\n\n---\n\nThat is the whole loop: **`jigc setup`** once, then **`jigc start \"<intent>\"` →\npick from the menu → `jigc start --workflow <chosen> \"<intent>\"` → implement →\n`jigc task finalize <id>`** per task. See\n`design/worked-examples.md` for end-to-end flows\nand `implementation/roadmap.md` for the build\nsequence.\n# Migrating to jigc — field notes\n\nTwo different migrations get called \"migrating to jigc.\" Both have now been run for real on a brownfield repo (the RC adoption trial, 2026-07-09 → 07-12); these notes distill what worked and what bites. They are working notes, not polished docs — updated as trials land.\n\n1. **Adopting an existing project** — foreign docs (a `.planning/` tree, loose Markdown, an old spec system) become jigc-managed docs. Verb: `jigc ingest` to classify, `jigc migrate <path> --as <doctype>` per doc.\n2. **Upgrading a jigc corpus across versions** — committed managed docs move to a new doctype schema-version. Verb: `jigc migrate-corpus`.\n\n## Adopting an existing project\n\nThe full method write-up from the reference migration (220 foreign files → 41 managed docs, ~55 commits, one day) is archived at completions/artifacts/RC-adoption/rerun-rc4/migration-method.md. The short version:\n\n1. **Triage with the tool first.** `jigc ingest` classifies every candidate against the managed schemas before you decide anything. Conformant-but-misplaced docs can be adopted; everything else is a re-authoring job through the write verbs. Knowing the split up front sets the whole plan's cost.\n2. **Treat every claim in the old docs as unverified.** This is the load-bearing rule, and it is why adoption is a *verification* job, not a reformatting job. Your existing docs may already be lying about the code — status claims especially (\"shipped\", \"passes audit\", \"the gate works\"). Check each claim against the code before carrying it over; a false claim migrated into jigc is *worse* than a deleted one, because the new system lends it credibility (managed, validated, green). Don't migrate what you haven't verified — and where a claim can't be cheaply verified, either drop it or carry it explicitly marked as unverified and put the verification question to the human.\n3. **Mine history for the four things worth keeping:** design decisions whose rationale isn't recoverable from the code, still-open debt, architecture knowledge, and work claimed complete that the code doesn't support. Everything else (plan summaries, validation reports, velocity tables) is process exhaust — drop it.\n4. **Put the genuine forks to the human** — not \"should I proceed\" but the actual decisions (delete vs mine-then-delete, is this doc still live, where does the backlog land).\n5. **Draft in parallel, write strictly sequentially.** Prose drafting and code-verification parallelize. Since rc.8 the *draft/author* half of bulk migration parallelizes even for docs sharing a directory: two same-directory sources whose slugs would collide today mint **distinct** task ids (a `blake3` of each source path is folded into the migration task id), so their `.jigc/tasks/<id>/` working areas no longer race the tool state. The jigc *writes* still run strictly sequentially — the git-index race is unchanged, each doc mints its own commit, so the finalize/commit path is one process, never parallel.\n6. **Author in dependency order.** Cross-referencing docs must land after their targets: ADRs before the arch-docs that cite them — finalize blocks a citation to a doc that doesn't exist.\n7. **Let `jigc migrate` retire each source in the same commit as its promoted doc.** The old tree drains itself; there is never a window where content exists in neither place. Then sweep for what the migration broke *outside* the corpus (READMEs pointing at the deleted tree, a stale CLAUDE.md).\n8. **Prefer block scalars (`key: |`) in author payloads** for any multi-paragraph prose (the templates demonstrate this since rc.5; double-quoted YAML folds newlines).\n\n## Upgrading a jigc corpus across versions\n\nVerified on the first real committed-corpus upgrade (rc.4 → rc.5, a 41-doc corpus, one doctype with an enum rename across 41 entries — byte-stable, zero prose touched, idempotent re-run). That trial found the *routing* around the upgrade silent, so the rc.6 wave hardened it; the steps below are the shipped surface.\n\n1. **Ask `jigc validate` whether you need to migrate — it blocks if you do.** A committed doc below its doctype's current schema-version raises `schema-conformance.schema-version-current`, routed at `jigc migrate-corpus`, and takes the exit code **non-zero**. The discriminator is each doc's own committed `schema-version` stamp, so the answer never depends on when the store was last stamped.\n2. **Run `jigc migrate-corpus` first, `jigc setup` second — and the tool now says so.** Over a stale corpus the `store-version.binary-mismatch` advisory routes at `migrate-corpus` first, and re-stamping the store no longer buys a green: `validate` still blocks on the per-doc stamps. Re-stamp with `jigc setup` once the corpus is current (with the corpus current, the advisory drops `migrate-corpus` from its route). *(Before rc.6 the ordering was yours to enforce — a re-stamped store over an unmigrated corpus validated clean.)*\n3. **Look first: `jigc migrate-corpus --dry-run`** prints the triage report the run *would* produce and changes nothing — no migrated bytes, no relocation move, no commit. Run the real thing on a clean tree, so the diff is the whole story.\n4. **The run lands its own commit.** `migrate-corpus` writes the migration and commits it, pathspec-limited to the paths it migrated (a half-landed migration — rewritten docs beside a removed relocation source, only some of them staged — is the failure mode that closes). `--no-commit` keeps the writes in the working tree instead, to review them or fold them into a larger commit yourself.\n5. **Audit what landed.** The transform is deterministic and the triage (`migrated` / `already_current` / `blocked` in `--format json`) tells you exactly what moved; the line accounting should close exactly (every changed line either a `schema-version` stamp or a declared transform). `blocked` docs are first-class output — they need a human or a prose rewrite, never a workaround.\n\n## Reconciling and backing out\n\nNothing in either migration is destructive until you say so. The gates, in the order you meet them (each verified against the shipped binary):\n\n1. **Look before landing: `jigc task finalize <id> --dry-run`.** It prints the manifest the finalize *would* commit — each path tagged by how it enters (`promoted` / `modified` / `deleted` / `carried-over`) — and stops. Nothing is committed, and on a migration task the forecast needs no `--approve`. It forecasts no green it would refuse: over a state the carryover gate below blocks, the dry-run emits those findings and takes the refusing exit instead of a manifest (declare the carry with `--carry-staged` and it forecasts it, labelled `carried-over`).\n2. **A plain finalize on a migration task holds for review.** `jigc task finalize <id>` without `--approve` renders the fidelity diff — the foreign source beside the canonical rewrite — and exits with nothing committed. The rewrite is the agent's; the CLI guarantees structure, never content-faithfulness, so this hold is where you read. **`--approve` is the sole destructive gate:** only the approving re-run writes the canonical doc, retires the foreign source, and commits.\n3. **A same-path migration lands `M`, never `D`+`A`.** When the foreign file already sits at the canonical destination (`jigc migrate docs/decisions/use-postgresql.md --as adr` — the common brownfield case), `--approve` rewrites it **in place**: one commit, `M <path>` in the log, nothing retired. Since rc.7 this holds for every doctype, not just singletons.\n4. **Pre-task staged changes refuse to ride: the carryover gate.** Minting a task snapshots the git index, and at finalize anything staged *before the task existed* refuses — one blocking `finalize.carried-staged` per carried path — instead of silently riding the task's commit. Unstage it (`git restore --staged -- <path>`) if it is not the task's work, or declare the carry deliberate with `--carry-staged`; the manifest then labels it `carried-over`. On a migration finalize `--carry-staged` composes with `--approve` — two independent declarations. You meet the gate before the commit boundary now: `jigc task validate <id>` and `jigc task finalize <id> --dry-run` run the same probe and refuse the same states, and both take `--carry-staged` as the same declaration.\n5. **A rejecting hook refuses the commit, and the message says how to recover.** jigc never passes `--no-verify` — your `pre-commit`/`commit-msg` hooks are policy — so a hook that exits non-zero stops the run and **nothing was committed** (HEAD does not move). What comes back is the hook's own stderr **verbatim**, a sentence naming what survived, and the exact line to re-run: fix the hook's complaint, run that line, and the commit lands. What survives differs per door and the message states *that door's* truth — `jigc task finalize <id>` leaves the task intact — its staged docs still in `.jigc/tasks/<id>/docs/`, anything you had `git add`-ed still in git's index, HEAD unmoved (the message names the two areas separately, because a docs-only task has nothing in git's index for you to check); `jigc migrate-corpus` leaves the migrated bytes written and staged, and its re-run **lands** them instead of reporting \"already current\"; a rejected `jigc milestone create` unwound both its record write and its workbench, so nothing of that milestone survives. All nine committing doors carry the frame, each with its own error code in the invocation log, so a failed run is identifiable afterwards.\n6. **Back out a task: `jigc task discard <id>`.** Workbench-only — it removes the task's working area (`.jigc/tasks/<id>/`) and touches nothing else: no commit, the committed store exactly as it was. **It refuses first while that area stages docs no commit has a copy of** — one blocking `task-discard.staged-prose` listing them, with the route to read them back (`jigc doc show <address> --task <id>`) or land them (`jigc task finalize <id>`); **`--force` is the single consent** that removes them with the area, and it is the same word `jigc uninstall` and `jigc milestone discard` take for the same reason. Minting a task stages its commit doc, so expect the refusal on any task you have started. A migration task discarded before `--approve` leaves the foreign source committed and untouched — abandoning costs nothing.\n7. **Back out a milestone: `jigc milestone discard <id>`.** The committed milestone record settles to `status: discarded` in one record-only commit — a genuinely joined sub-task stays `joined`; it really did land — and then the workbench (sub-task areas, fan-out worktrees) tears down. A sub-task worktree holding uncommitted work refuses the discard unless `--force`.\n8. **Hand edits are detected and routed — never silently merged, never forbidden.** Editing a committed managed doc outside the loop is expected (it is plain Markdown in git). `jigc validate` names the drifted path with a route to re-author it through the owning workflow; the report is exit-0 at store scope and gates at `jigc task validate` / `jigc task finalize`.\n\n## What migration does *not* give you\n\n- **Truthful prose.** jigc's gates keep docs naming *real code* (anchors, refs, structure); they do not detect that a paragraph's *claim* went stale. A migrated corpus is only as honest as the verification you did at step 2 above — and it starts rotting again the first time a fix lands without its doc pass.\n- **Linear-time authoring of a very large document.** Every `migrate-*` workflow hands `jigc doc author` the whole document in **one** payload, and the apply chain re-reads the growing buffer once per lowered leaf — so the cost is **quadratic in the item count**, not linear, and that residual is the shipped bound. It used to be worse: the read itself was quadratic too, making the batch cubic (an 800-item document took 77 s to author, a 1500-item one never finished). That factor is gone — the same 800-item document now takes under 7 s, 1500 items under 30 s — but quadratic is still quadratic: a single source document with several thousand items is minutes, not seconds. The batch is atomic, so a long run is a long run and never a partial one; if a foreign document is enormous, splitting it at a real boundary beats waiting.\n\n- **A finished doc set.** Migration carries what exists. The gaps the old system had (undocumented decisions, missing specs) are still gaps — now visible ones."}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a159fced-22f2-4824-996a-1302182fbd40","uuid":"a3f85c4b-89ef-4b8e-b333-abad58adcc07","timestamp":"2026-09-09T21:21:12.804Z","isSynthetic":true}

To cut a release, mint a release item (its id is minted\\nfrom the version title you give — e.g. `1.0.0` mints `1-0-0` — and its `date` is\\nstamped on create). `add-item` PRINTS the new item's address; use that printed\\naddress verbatim for every follow-up verb — never re-spell the version string:\\n\\njigc doc add-item changelog:changelog#releases --title \\\"<version>\\\" --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\n#   → prints e.g. changelog:changelog#releases/1-0-0  (call this <release-addr>)\\njigc doc set-field <release-addr>/link --value \\\"<diff-url>\\\" --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\n\\nEvery `--from-file -` below reads its payload from stdin — attach it as a heredoc\\ndirectly to the `jigc` command, never as a `cat payload | jigc …` pipeline, which\\njigc itself accepts but an agent harness that statically analyses shell commands\\ncan refuse to run:\\n\\njigc doc set-slot <group-addr>/notes --from-file - --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so <<'EOF'\\n<one bullet per change>\\nEOF\\n\\nUnder that release, add one nested change-group per category and author its notes\\n(one bullet per change). The category is the group's id (added / changed /\\ndeprecated / removed / fixed / security). Over an already-committed changelog it copies\\nthe committed doc in and appends — existing releases are untouched; an entry whose\\ntitle mints an id the changelog already holds is refused (`write.already-present`),\\nthe WHOLE payload rejected and nothing staged, so edit that entry in place with the\\nper-entry `set-field` / `set-slot` lines above rather than re-authoring it here:\\n\\njigc doc author changelog --from-file - --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\n\\nRead your write back before you move on — with `--task` the read serves THIS\\ntask's staged copy, the write you just made, which the committed store does not\\ncarry yet:\\n\\njigc doc show changelog:changelog --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\n\\nWhen done, set the required Conventional-Commits type — your editorial call on\\nwhat this change does. The subject renders as `<type>(<scope>): <summary>`, so\\nwrite the summary without a type or scope prefix of its own — the `type` field\\nalready carries it. Inside slot prose, the reserved heading depths are schema-relative to\\nthe address you write — the CLI owns the section, item, and sub-label heading\\nlevels there, so your headings sit below them; Setext headings are rejected at\\nevery depth, and a rejected write names the shallowest depth free at that\\naddress.\\n\\nEvery `--from-file -` below reads its payload from stdin — attach it as a heredoc\\ndirectly to the `jigc` command, never as a `cat payload | jigc …` pipeline, which\\njigc itself accepts but an agent harness that statically analyses shell commands\\ncan refuse to run:\\n\\njigc doc set-slot commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#summary --from-file - --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so <<'EOF'\\n<one line saying what changed>\\nEOF\\n\\nSet the type, then stage the summary prose:\\n\\nRun: `jigc doc set-field commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#type --value <COMMIT_TYPE> --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so`\\nThe `type` value is one of: feat | fix | docs | style | refactor | perf | test | build | ci | chore | revert\\nRun: `jigc doc set-slot commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#summary --from-file - --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so`\\n<<author: commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#summary>>\\n\\nThe `commit` schema is the authority on what you write into it — its required\\nslots and fields, each field's enum members, and every address a write can take:\\n\\njigc doc schema commit\\n\\nThe `scope` and `body` are optional: add a `scope` to name the area touched, or\\nauthor a `body` to explain the motivation, only when they earn their place —\\n\\njigc doc set-field commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#scope --value <area> --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\njigc doc set-slot commit:changelog-the-work-shipped-so#body --from-file - --task changelog-the-work-shipped-so\\n\\nWhen the work shares authorship — a co-author, or an agent that wrote it — record\\nit in a commit trailer. What\nneither mechanism bounds is the number of distinct series — the number of keys\nin the `buckets` map itself. Every never-seen-before series name allocates a\nnew bucket, and that bucket survives until every sample in it ages past\nretention. A single mislabeled writer that folds a high-cardinality value\n(a user id, a request id) into the series name, or a burst of one-off labels,\ncan mint unboundedly many buckets faster than retention ever expires them —\ngrowing the map until the process runs out of memory. This decision was left\nopen when the store shipped ([[drop-the-oldest-sample]] bounds sample loss\nwithin a series and within the ingest queue, but says nothing about the\nseries-count axis).\n\nThe fix follows the precedent already set for sample-level overflow: under\nmemory pressure, a recent picture beats a stale one, so the store should shed\nits least-recently-touched series rather than refuse a newly-seen one. This\nalso matches the failure mode: a runaway label is, by construction, series\nthis store has never seen touched recently by legitimate traffic, so eviction\nby recency naturally targets the label doing the damage while sparing series\nreceiving steady, expected traffic.\n\nNon-goals: this does not change the per-series sample cap (`maxPerSeries`) or\nthe ingest queue's overflow policy — both already exist and are out of scope.\nIt does not add persistence, back-pressure on writers, or a way to reclaim a\nspecific evicted series; once evicted, a series' history is gone and a later\nsample for it starts a fresh bucket. It does not change `prune()`'s\nretention-based cleanup, which still runs independently.\n\nExpected to touch: `src/store.ts` (`MemoryStore` — the bound and eviction),\n`src/config.ts` (`Config`/`loadConfig` — a new `maxSeries` setting), and\n`src/index.ts` (`createService` — wiring the new config value into the store's\nconstructor).\nEOF"},"caller":{"type":"direct"}}],"container":null,"stop_reason":null,"stop_sequence":null,"stop_details":null,"usage":{"input_tokens":2,"cache_creation_input_tokens":313,"cache_read_input_tokens":85655,"cache_creation":{"ephemeral_5m_input_tokens":0,"ephemeral_1h_input_tokens":313},"output_tokens":20,"service_tier":"standard","inference_geo":"not_available"},"diagnostics":null,"context_management":null},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a159fced-22f2-4824-996a-1302182fbd40","uuid":"65151ca6-8fea-4475-9606-a2085219e814","timestamp":"2026-09-09T21:23:23.244Z","request_id":"req_011CetZwYNxprtUMsn3eyeiX"}

It is deliberately not a database — every\ncomponent in the pipeline exists to hold a *recent* picture cheaply, and every\none of them sheds data under pressure rather than growing without bound or\nblocking a writer.\n\nThe pipeline has two halves that meet at `Service.tick()` (src/index.ts):\n\n  wire line → validate → IngestQueue → [tick] → MemoryStore → rollup → summary\n\nA `POST /samples` line is parsed and validated, then pushed onto the\n`IngestQueue` — nothing is queryable yet. `tick()` drains the queue into the\n`MemoryStore` and prunes anything past retention; a `GET /summary/<series>`\nreads the store, folds the matching window(s) through `rollup`, and reduces\nthem to one `Summary` line through `summarize`/`formatSummary`. Everything\ndownstream of the queue only ever sees samples that already passed validation;\neverything downstream of the store only ever sees samples that already\nsurvived the queue's and the store's own capacity bounds.\n\nThe system leans on a small set of injected primitives — `Config`\n(src/config.ts) and `Clock` (src/clock.ts) — so window alignment, retention,\nand capacity are all deterministic and testable without touching real time or\nenvironment state.\n\nBounded by construction, on three independent axes: the ingest queue caps how\nmany samples can be buffered before landing in the store (`maxSamples`,\noldest-dropped on overflow); the store caps how many samples one series can\nhold (`maxSamples` again, oldest-dropped) *and* how many distinct series it\nwill track at once (`maxSeries`, least-recently-touched series evicted); and\n`prune()` independently ages out anything past `retentionMs`. It is deliberately not a database — every\\ncomponent in the pipeline exists to hold a *recent* picture cheaply, and every\\none of them sheds data under pressure rather than growing without bound or\\nblocking a writer.\\n\\nThe pipeline has two halves that meet at `Service.tick()` (src/index.ts):\\n\\n  wire line → validate → IngestQueue → [tick] → MemoryStore → rollup → summary\\n\\nA `POST /samples` line is parsed and validated, then pushed onto the\\n`IngestQueue` — nothing is queryable yet. `tick()` drains the queue into the\\n`MemoryStore` and prunes anything past retention; a `GET /summary/<series>`\\nreads the store, folds the matching window(s) through `rollup`, and reduces\\nthem to one `Summary` line through `summarize`/`formatSummary`. Everything\\ndownstream of the queue only ever sees samples that already passed validation;\\neverything downstream of the store only ever sees samples that already\\nsurvived the queue's and the store's own capacity bounds.\\n\\nThe system leans on a small set of injected primitives — `Config`\\n(src/config.ts) and `Clock` (src/clock.ts) — so window alignment, retention,\\nand capacity are all deterministic and testable without touching real time or\\nenvironment state.\\n\\nBounded by construction, on three independent axes: the ingest queue caps how\\nmany samples can be buffered before landing in the store (`maxSamples`,\\noldest-dropped on overflow); the store caps how many samples one series can\\nhold (`maxSamples` again, oldest-dropped) *and* how many distinct series it\\nwill track at once (`maxSeries`, least-recently-touched series evicted); and\\n`prune()` independently ages out anything past `retentionMs`. Losing data is\\nnever silent — the queue counts `droppedCount()`, the store counts\\n`evictedSeriesCount()` — but under sustained pressure this service always\\nprefers a smaller, current picture to a frozen or unbounded one.\"\n  },\n  \"slug\": \"ingest-store-and-rollup\",\n  \"staged\": \"how-ingest-store-and-rollup\",\n  \"type\": \"arch-doc\"\n}","is_error":false}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"a159fced-22f2-4824-996a-1302182fbd40","uuid":"1747191c-0060-4f95-8288-d9b664572f81","timestamp":"2026-09-09T21:27:58.307Z","tool_use_result":{"stdout":"{\n  \"fields\": {\n    \"cites\": [\n      \"adr:drop-the-oldest-sample\",\n      \"adr:keep-the-sample-store\"\n    ],\n    \"schema-version\": \"1\"\n  },\n  \"item-count\": 6,\n  \"schema-version\": 1,\n  \"sections\": {\n    \"components\": [\n      {\n        \"description\": \"The two primitives every other component is built against. `Config` is read\\nonce from the environment at startup — window width, per-series sample cap,\\nseries-count cap, retention — with a validated, defaulted `loadConfig()` so a\\npresent-but-malformed value fails loudly instead of falling back silently.\\n`Clock` abstracts `now()` so window alignment and retention sweeps take time as\\nan explicit input; `SystemClock` reads the wall clock in production and\\n`FixedClock` lets tests move time deliberately.\",\n        \"id\": \"config-and-clock\",\n        \"implemented-by\": \"src/config.ts#loadConfig\",\n        \"title\": \"Config and Clock\"\n      },\n      {\n        \"description\": \"The one place a sample is admitted or rejected. `validateSample` coerces an\\nunknown payload into a well-formed `Sample` (a valid series name, a finite\\nvalue, an epoch timestamp) or throws a `ValidationError` naming the offending\\nfield — never a partially valid object. It is deliberately not a database — every\\ncomponent in the pipeline exists to hold a *recent* picture cheaply, and every\\none of them sheds data under pressure rather than growing without bound or\\nblocking a writer.\\n\\nThe pipeline has two halves that meet at `Service.tick()` (src/index.ts):\\n\\n  wire line → validate → IngestQueue → [tick] → MemoryStore → rollup → summary\\n\\nA `POST /samples` line is parsed and validated, then pushed onto the\\n`IngestQueue` — nothing is queryable yet. `tick()` drains the queue into the\\n`MemoryStore` and prunes anything past retention; a `GET /summary/<series>`\\nreads the store, folds the matching window(s) through `rollup`, and reduces\\nthem to one `Summary` line through `summarize`/`formatSummary`. Everything\\ndownstream of the queue only ever sees samples that already passed validation;\\neverything downstream of the store only ever sees samples that already\\nsurvived the queue's and the store's own capacity bounds.\\n\\nThe system leans on a small set of injected primitives — `Config`\\n(src/config.ts) and `Clock` (src/clock.ts) — so window alignment, retention,\\nand capacity are all deterministic and testable without touching real time or\\nenvironment state.\\n\\nBounded by construction, on three independent axes: the ingest queue caps how\\nmany samples can be buffered before landing in the store (`maxSamples`,\\noldest-dropped on overflow); the store caps how many samples one series can\\nhold (`maxSamples` again, oldest-dropped) *and* how many distinct series it\\nwill track at once (`maxSeries`, least-recently-touched series evicted); and\\n`prune()` independently ages out anything past `retentionMs`. Losing data is\\nnever silent — the queue counts `droppedCount()`, the store counts\\n`evictedSeriesCount()` — but under sustained pressure this service always\\nprefers a smaller, current picture to a frozen or unbounded one.\"\n  },\n  \"slug\": \"ingest-store-and-rollup\",\n  \"staged\": \"how-ingest-store-and-rollup\",\n  \"type\": \"arch-doc\"\n}","stderr":"","interrupted":false,"isImage":false,"noOutputExpected":false}}
